Although she was not physically injured, she was close enough to see the impact and stayed with the teens, trying to comfort them until emergency services arrived, later replaying the chaotic scene as she gave statements to police and local reporters. In the months that followed, she gradually withdrew from leading tours and avoided the intersection, but the impact of the incident seeped into other cherished routines at the senior center and church, straining her sense of purpose and connection. She finally agreed to see a mental health professional at the urging of her pastor and a longtime friend after they noticed she had stopped attending book club and bowling, and she admitted she could no longer enjoy the community activities that once defined her retirement.\n- Chief Complaint: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): Before the accident I lived for the walking tours, the garden, even silly little things like bowling night, but now when people invite me I just feel blank inside, like there’s no point in going because I know I won’t enjoy it the way I used to.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): I used to be sharp enough to remember all the dates and stories for the tours, but these days I lose my train of thought in the middle of a conversation or stand in the kitchen staring at the pantry because I can’t decide something as simple as what to make for dinner.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere, especially when I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I’m right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, hearing the thud and the screaming so vividly that I feel sick and shaky even though I know I’m just standing on my own sidewalk.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go the long way around town so I don’t have to pass that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if someone brings up car accidents or asks me about my old tour route, because even thinking about it makes my chest tighten.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep thinking that if I’d moved the group a little faster or insisted we wait for the next light, that boy might still be alive, and I can’t shake the feeling that I failed them, even when people tell me there was nothing more I could have done.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most days I walk around with this heavy mix of guilt and dread sitting in my stomach, like a dark cloud that doesn’t lift even when I’m at church or with my grandchildren, and it’s hard to remember what it felt like to be genuinely happy or at ease.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I make myself go to church or the senior center, I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else laugh and chat, and I just can’t seem to connect with them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even though I know that’s not really how I feel deep down.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I catch myself constantly checking the street before I step off the curb, watching cars like a hawk and tensing at every engine rev or loud noise, and even when I’m just sitting in a café I like to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped leading tours, dropped out of most of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving, so my days have shrunk down to mostly sitting at home alone and avoiding situations that used to give my life structure and meaning.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me ever since that truck hit those kids at the crosswalk a little over two years ago, and they really haven’t let up since.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and Although she was not physically injured, she was close enough to see the impact and stayed with the teens, trying to comfort them until emergency services arrived, later replaying the chaotic scene as she gave statements to police and local reporters. In the months that followed, she gradually withdrew from leading tours and avoided the intersection, but the impact of the incident seeped into other cherished routines at the senior center and church, straining her sense of purpose and connection. She finally agreed to see a mental health professional at the urging of her pastor and a longtime friend after they noticed she had stopped attending book club and bowling, and she admitted she could no longer enjoy the community activities that once defined her retirement.\n- Chief Complaint: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): Before the accident I lived for the walking tours, the garden, even silly little things like bowling night, but now when people invite me I just feel blank inside, like there’s no point in going because I know I won’t enjoy it the way I used to.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): I used to be sharp enough to remember all the dates and stories for the tours, but these days I lose my train of thought in the middle of a conversation or stand in the kitchen staring at the pantry because I can’t decide something as simple as what to make for dinner.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere, especially when I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I’m right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, hearing the thud and the screaming so vividly that I feel sick and shaky even though I know I’m just standing on my own sidewalk.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go the long way around town so I don’t have to pass that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if someone brings up car accidents or asks me about my old tour route, because even thinking about it makes my chest tighten.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep thinking that if I’d moved the group a little faster or insisted we wait for the next light, that boy might still be alive, and I can’t shake the feeling that I failed them, even when people tell me there was nothing more I could have done.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most days I walk around with this heavy mix of guilt and dread sitting in my stomach, like a dark cloud that doesn’t lift even when I’m at church or with my grandchildren, and it’s hard to remember what it felt like to be genuinely happy or at ease.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I make myself go to church or the senior center, I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else laugh and chat, and I just can’t seem to connect with them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even though I know that’s not really how I feel deep down.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I catch myself constantly checking the street before I step off the curb, watching cars like a hawk and tensing at every engine rev or loud noise, and even when I’m just sitting in a café I like to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped leading tours, dropped out of most of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving, so my days have shrunk down to mostly sitting at home alone and avoiding situations that used to give my life structure and meaning.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me ever since that truck hit those kids at the crosswalk a little over have to pass that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if someone brings up car accidents or asks me about my old tour route, because even thinking about it makes my chest tighten.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep thinking that if I’d moved the group a little faster or insisted we wait for the next light, that boy might still be alive, and I can’t shake the feeling that I failed them, even when people tell me there was nothing more I could have done.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most days I walk around with this heavy mix of guilt and dread sitting in my stomach, like a dark cloud that doesn’t lift even when I’m at church or with my grandchildren, and it’s hard to remember what it felt like to be genuinely happy or at ease.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I make myself go to church or the senior center, I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else laugh and chat, and I just can’t seem to connect with them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even though I know that’s not really how I feel deep down.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I catch myself constantly checking the street before I step off the curb, watching cars like a hawk and tensing at every engine rev or loud noise, and even when I’m just sitting in a café I like to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped leading tours, dropped out of most of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving, so my days have shrunk down to mostly sitting at home alone and avoiding situations that used to give my life structure and meaning.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me ever since that truck hit those kids at the crosswalk a little over Although she was not physically injured, she was close enough to see the impact and stayed with the teens, trying to comfort them until emergency services arrived, later replaying the chaotic scene as she gave statements to police and local reporters. She finally agreed to see a mental health professional at the urging of her pastor and a longtime friend after they noticed she had stopped attending book club and bowling, and she admitted she could late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even Ever since that day, I constantly feel on edge watching cars, relive the crash when I hear screeching tires, and go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling emotionally numb or detached from other people, or any sudden outbursts of anger or irritability since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel terribly detached, like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone at church or the senior center without being able to connect. I've also been very irritable and snap at people over tiny things, like a friend being slightly late, which makes me feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to work, manage daily tasks, or maintain relationships, and if so how much have they interfered?"
  }

It's just a constant, wearying struggle.\n\nDoctor: How long have these symptoms of low mood, loss of interest, guilt, and poor concentration been going on continuously?\n\nPatient: They've been going on continuously for a little over two years now, ever since that terrible accident at the crosswalk. They really haven't let up at all since then.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you have unwanted memories, nightmares, flashbacks, or do you try to avoid reminders of it because they bring up intense distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I hear tires screech or see a truck, I'm right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, which makes me feel completely sick and shaky. I go the long way around town to avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone brings up the accident because it makes my chest tighten.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you also feel constantly on edge or easily startled, have trouble sleeping because of it, or find yourself being unusually watchful for danger?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am constantly on edge and incredibly watchful now. I watch cars like a hawk and tense up at every loud engine rev, always checking the street before stepping off the curb. Even when I'm just sitting in a café, I have to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after the accident and include intrusive memories, avoidance, and feeling constantly on edge, or did they start for no clear reason and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: These symptoms absolutely started right after that tragic accident two years ago, rather than for no reason. Ever since that day, I constantly feel on edge watching cars, relive the crash when I hear screeching tires, and go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling emotionally numb or detached from other people, or any sudden outbursts of anger or irritability since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel terribly detached, like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone at church or the senior center without being able to connect. I've also been very irritable and snap at people over tiny things, like a friend being slightly late, which makes me feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to work, manage daily tasks, or maintain relationships, and if so how much have they interfered?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have completely taken over my life and disrupted everything. I've stopped leading my walking tours, dropped out of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving. My world has shrunk to mostly sitting at home alone, and I've lost all the structure and meaning I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident and these symptoms started, have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't have active plans to hurt myself, but on nights when the memories won't quiet down, I drink more wine than I should and drive too fast on back roads, thinking, \"Who cares what happens to me anyway.\" Deep down, I know that's not how I really feel, but in those moments, I feel a reckless disregard for my own safety."

I've also been very irritable and snap at people over tiny things, like a friend being slightly late, which makes me feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to work, manage daily tasks, or maintain relationships, and if so how much have they interfered?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have completely taken over my life and disrupted everything. I've stopped leading my walking tours, dropped out of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving. My world has shrunk to mostly sitting at home alone, and I've lost all the structure and meaning I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident and these symptoms started, have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't have active plans to hurt myself, but on nights when the memories won't quiet down, I drink more wine than I should and drive too fast on back roads, thinking, \"Who cares what happens to me anyway.\" Deep down, I know that's not how I really feel, but in those moments, I feel a reckless disregard for my own safety.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin only after the accident and stay tied to reminders of it, or did they start independently of the accident and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Everything definitely started right after the accident two years ago and hasn't let up since. It is really a mix of both: specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those sudden, intense reactions, but the heavy cloud of guilt, dread, and loss of interest is constant and stays with me almost all day, every single day."

I've stopped leading my walking tours, dropped out of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving. My world has shrunk to mostly sitting at home alone, and I've lost all the structure and meaning I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident and these symptoms started, have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't have active plans to hurt myself, but on nights when the memories won't quiet down, I drink more wine than I should and drive too fast on back roads, thinking, \"Who cares what happens to me anyway.\" Deep down, I know that's not how I really feel, but in those moments, I feel a reckless disregard for my own safety.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin only after the accident and stay tied to reminders of it, or did they start independently of the accident and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Everything definitely started right after the accident two years ago and hasn't let up since. It is really a mix of both: specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those sudden, intense reactions, but the heavy cloud of guilt, dread, and loss of interest is constant and stays with me almost all day, every single day.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin only after the accident and get triggered by reminders of it, or did they start without any clear trigger and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: They definitely started right after the accident and have been with me ever since. It is really a mix of both, because while specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those intense panic reactions, I also carry a heavy cloud of guilt and dread that stays with me almost every single day."
  }

Ever since that day, I constantly feel on edge watching cars, relive the crash when I hear screeching tires, and go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling emotionally numb or detached from other people, or any sudden outbursts of anger or irritability since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel terribly detached, like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone at church or the senior center without being able to connect. I've also been very irritable and snap at people over tiny things, like a friend being slightly late, which makes me feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to work, manage daily tasks, or maintain relationships, and if so how much have they interfered?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have completely taken over my life and disrupted everything. I've stopped leading my walking tours, dropped out of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving. My world has shrunk to mostly sitting at home alone, and I've lost all the structure and meaning I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident and these symptoms started, have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't have active plans to hurt myself, but on nights when the memories won't quiet down, I drink more wine than I should and drive too fast on back roads, thinking, \"Who cares what happens to me anyway.\" Deep down, I know that's not how I really feel, but in those moments, I feel a reckless disregard for my own safety.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin only after the accident and stay tied to reminders of it, or did they start independently of the accident and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Everything definitely started right after the accident two years ago and hasn't let up since. It is really a mix of both: specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those sudden, intense reactions, but the heavy cloud of guilt, dread, and loss of interest is constant and stays with me almost all day, every single day.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin only after the accident and get triggered by reminders of it, or did they start without any clear trigger and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: They definitely started right after the accident and have been with me ever since. It is really a mix of both, because while specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those intense panic reactions, I also carry a heavy cloud of guilt and dread that stays with me almost every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been mainly tied to the accident and reminders of it, or have you also had a separate period of low mood and loss of interest that lasted at least two weeks without being related to the trauma?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any separate periods like that at all. Everything—the low mood, the loss of interest, and the constant distress—started directly after the accident and has been ongoing ever since. Before that day, I never struggled with anything like this."

They really haven't let up at all since then.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you have unwanted memories, nightmares, flashbacks, or do you try to avoid reminders of it because they bring up intense distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I hear tires screech or see a truck, I'm right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, which makes me feel completely sick and shaky. I go the long way around town to avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone brings up the accident because it makes my chest tighten.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you also feel constantly on edge or easily startled, have trouble sleeping because of it, or find yourself being unusually watchful for danger?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am constantly on edge and incredibly watchful now. I watch cars like a hawk and tense up at every loud engine rev, always checking the street before stepping off the curb. Even when I'm just sitting in a café, I have to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after the accident and include intrusive memories, avoidance, and feeling constantly on edge, or did they start for no clear reason and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: These symptoms absolutely started right after that tragic accident two years ago, rather than for no reason. Ever since that day, I constantly feel on edge watching cars, relive the crash when I hear screeching tires, and go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling emotionally numb or detached from other people, or any sudden outbursts of anger or irritability since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel terribly detached, like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone at church or the senior center without being able to connect. I've also been very irritable and snap at people over tiny things, like a friend being slightly late, which makes me feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to work, manage daily tasks, or maintain relationships, and if so how much have they interfered?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have completely taken over my life and disrupted everything. I've stopped leading my walking tours, dropped out of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving. My world has shrunk to mostly sitting at home alone, and I've lost all the structure and meaning I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident and these symptoms started, have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't have active plans to hurt myself, but on nights when the memories won't quiet down, I drink more wine than I should and drive too fast on back roads, thinking, \"Who cares what happens to me anyway.\" Deep down, I know that's not how I really feel, but in those moments, I feel a reckless disregard for my own safety.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin only after the accident and stay tied to reminders of it, or did they start independently of the accident and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Everything definitely started right after the accident two years ago and hasn't let up since. It is really a mix of both: specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those sudden, intense reactions, but the heavy cloud of guilt, dread, and loss of interest is constant and stays with me almost all day, every single day.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin only after the accident and get triggered by reminders of it, or did they start without any clear trigger and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: They definitely started right after the accident and have been with me ever since. It is really a mix of both, because while specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those intense panic reactions, I also carry a heavy cloud of guilt and dread that stays with me almost every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been mainly tied to the accident and reminders of it, or have you also had a separate period of low mood and loss of interest that lasted at least two weeks without being related to the trauma?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any separate periods like that at all. Everything—the low mood, the loss of interest, and the constant distress—started directly after the accident and has been ongoing ever since. Before that day, I never struggled with anything like this.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for less than a month after the accident, or have they continued for more than a month?\n\nPatient: They have been present for much longer than a month. These symptoms have been going on continuously for over two years, ever since the accident occurred, and they really haven't let up at all."

It feels like I've completely lost my connection to the things that used to give my life purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar period of depression or loss of interest like this before, or is this the first time it has happened?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before. This is the first time in my life I've felt so completely disconnected, and it really only started after that terrible accident. Before that, I was always very active and happy.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also been feeling down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or having changes in sleep, appetite, energy, or concentration?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly carry this heavy mix of guilt and dread, like a dark cloud that makes it impossible to feel happy or at ease anymore. My concentration has also been terrible—I often lose my train of thought mid-conversation and struggle to make simple daily decisions, like what to cook for dinner. It's just a constant, wearying struggle.\n\nDoctor: How long have these symptoms of low mood, loss of interest, guilt, and poor concentration been going on continuously?\n\nPatient: They've been going on continuously for a little over two years now, ever since that terrible accident at the crosswalk. They really haven't let up at all since then.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you have unwanted memories, nightmares, flashbacks, or do you try to avoid reminders of it because they bring up intense distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I hear tires screech or see a truck, I'm right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, which makes me feel completely sick and shaky. I go the long way around town to avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone brings up the accident because it makes my chest tighten.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you also feel constantly on edge or easily startled, have trouble sleeping because of it, or find yourself being unusually watchful for danger?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am constantly on edge and incredibly watchful now. I watch cars like a hawk and tense up at every loud engine rev, always checking the street before stepping off the curb. Even when I'm just sitting in a café, I have to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after the accident and include intrusive memories, avoidance, and feeling constantly on edge, or did they start for no clear reason and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: These symptoms absolutely started right after that tragic accident two years ago, rather than for no reason. Ever since that day, I constantly feel on edge watching cars, relive the crash when I hear screeching tires, and go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling emotionally numb or detached from other people, or any sudden outbursts of anger or irritability since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel terribly detached, like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone at church or the senior center without being able to connect. I've also been very irritable and snap at people over tiny things, like a friend being slightly late, which makes me feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been making it hard for you to work, manage daily tasks, or maintain relationships, and if so how much have they interfered?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have completely taken over my life and disrupted everything. I've stopped leading my walking tours, dropped out of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving. My world has shrunk to mostly sitting at home alone, and I've lost all the structure and meaning I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident and these symptoms started, have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I don't have active plans to hurt myself, but on nights when the memories won't quiet down, I drink more wine than I should and drive too fast on back roads, thinking, \"Who cares what happens to me anyway.\" Deep down, I know that's not how I really feel, but in those moments, I feel a reckless disregard for my own safety.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin only after the accident and stay tied to reminders of it, or did they start independently of the accident and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Everything definitely started right after the accident two years ago and hasn't let up since. It is really a mix of both: specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those sudden, intense reactions, but the heavy cloud of guilt, dread, and loss of interest is constant and stays with me almost all day, every single day.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems begin only after the accident and get triggered by reminders of it, or did they start without any clear trigger and persist most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: They definitely started right after the accident and have been with me ever since. It is really a mix of both, because while specific reminders like screeching tires trigger those intense panic reactions, I also carry a heavy cloud of guilt and dread that stays with me almost every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been mainly tied to the accident and reminders of it, or have you also had a separate period of low mood and loss of interest that lasted at least two weeks without being related to the trauma?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any separate periods like that at all. Everything—the low mood, the loss of interest, and the constant distress—started directly after the accident and has been ongoing ever since. Before that day, I never struggled with anything like this.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for less than a month after the accident, or have they continued for more than a month?\n\nPatient: They have been present for much longer than a month. These symptoms have been going on continuously for over two years, ever since the accident occurred, and they really haven't let up at all.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms start within a month after the accident and stay tied to reminders of it, or did they begin much later or without a clear link to the trauma?\n\nPatient: Yes, they started almost immediately after that terrible night and have been hanging over me ever since. They are very much tied to reminders, like hearing tires screech, seeing a truck near a crosswalk, or even just thinking about that intersection."

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
